1 
Panel’s proposed programs.  In brief, we address: 
2 
 Heating Electrification Make-Ready Program (“HEMR”): 
3

4 
Energy Programs Panel (“SCEPP”) maintains that the 
5 
HEMR should be considered in the New Efficiency: New 
6 
York (“NENY”) proceeding rather than in this rate 
7 
case. Natural Resources Defense Council (“NRDC”), WE 
8 
ACT for Environmental Justice and Alliance for a 
9 
Green Economy (“WE ACT AGREE”), and BlocPower also 
10 
discuss the HEMR. 
11 
 Customer Recommendation and Analysis (“CR&A”) Tools: 
12 
SCEPP recommends removing the proposed project. 
13 
 Distributed Energy Resources (“DER”) Make-Ready 
14 
Program for Disadvantaged Communities (“DAC”) and 
15 
Low-Income Customers: SCEPP recommends removing the 
16 
proposed Make-Ready Program. 
17 
 Clean Energy Credit for Low-Income Customers: Parties 
18 
oppose the program, argue that it should be 
19 
considered in a different proceeding, or propose 
20 
alternative program designs. 
21

II. 
Supporting Customer Participation in the Clean Energy 
15 
Transition 
16 
A. Heating Electrification Make-Ready Program  
17 
Q. 
Please describe Staff’s testimony regarding the Company’s 
18 
Heating Electrification Make-Ready Program (“HEMR”) 
19 
Program proposal.   
20 


--- Page 9 ---
CASES 22-E-0064 & 22-G-0065 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
9 
 
A. 
Although Staff (SCEPP, pp. 15-21) supports the program, it 
1 
asserts that it should be considered in the NENY 
2 
proceeding as part of the Interim Review.    
3 
Q. 
Do you agree? 
4 
A. 
No.  The HEMR is intended to assist customers in older 
5 
buildings overcome the cost barrier of behind-the-meter 
6 
electrical upgrades required to adopt heat pumps. Moving 
7 
this program to the NENY proceeding may delay 
8 
implementation and hinder Climate Leadership and Community 
9 
Protection Act (“CLCPA”) goals.   
10 
Q. 
Please explain. 
11 
A. 
If the Commission approves the HEMR as part of a rate 
12 
order or rate settlement, it will likely occur in early 
13 
2023.  The Company will then start the program shortly 
14 
thereafter. Moving the HEMR to the NENY proceeding for 
15 
consideration in the Interim Review would likely delay 
16 
approval until early 2024. Additionally, once approved as 
17 
a statewide program, the HEMR would require implementation 
18 
through the statewide Joint Management Committee, 
19 
potentially delaying implementation.     
20 
Q. 
What are the benefits to an earlier start? 
21 


--- Page 10 ---
CASES 22-E-0064 & 22-G-0065 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
10 
 
A. 
Meeting CLCPA goals requires a rapid increase in the pace 
1 
of heating electrification adoption across all customer 
2 
segments. The Governor’s goal of electrifying a million 
3 
homes and making an additional one million homes 
4 
electrification-ready by 2030 requires an increase in 
5 
pace.  The earlier start would bring the State of New York 
6 
(“State”) closer to its goal and benefit customers by 
7 
providing incentives for electrical upgrades in an 
8 
estimated 2,750 homes, 250 multifamily buildings, and 250 
9 
commercial properties sooner. Additionally, the Company’s 
10 
experience running the program starting in early 2023 
11 
would allow the Company to develop insights that would 
12 
productively inform any future statewide programs as part 
13 
of the NENY Interim Review.  
14 
Q. 
Turning to other parties, do you agree with BlocPower’s 
15 
suggestion (pp. 9-10) that the Company should change the 
16 
program funding allocated to multifamily buildings to 
17 
support electrification of 5,000 multifamily homes? 
18 
A. 
No.  The Company’s current proposal is correctly scaled.  
19 
It would electrify space or water heating for more than 
20 
30,000 dwelling units in 2,500 existing multifamily 
21 


--- Page 11 ---
CASES 22-E-0064 & 22-G-0065 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
11 
 
buildings, using 48 percent of the proposed incentive 
1 
budget.  This target already accelerates existing 
2 
multifamily building electrification, which has, to date, 
3 
seen less interest than other categories.     
4 
Q. 
Has the Company set an LMI-specific target for the HEMR as 
5 
requested by BlocPower (p. 10)? 
6 
A. 
Yes.  The Company’s target is that a minimum of 20 percent 
7 
of residential sector HEMR investment be in LMI-qualifying 
8 
buildings, including both 1-4 family homes and multifamily 
9 
buildings.  
10 
Q. 
How does the Company respond to WE ACT AGREE’s position 
11 
(Jessel, p. 45) that the CLCPA requires 40 percent of HEMR 
12 
investment to take place in DACs? 
13 
A. 
The Company disagrees.  Please see the rebuttal testimony 
14 
of the Company’s CLCPA Panel for more detail on the 
15 
Company’s position.    
16 
Q. 
Does the Company agree with WE ACT AGREE’s position 
17 
(Jessel, p. 59) that the HEMR should provide free 
18 
induction cooking appliances to renters in participating 
19 
buildings that are currently non-heating gas customers? 
20 


--- Page 12 ---
CASES 22-E-0064 & 22-G-0065 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
12 
 
A. 
No.  The Company CLCPA Panel’s rebuttal testimony explains 
1 
that the Company does not believe that the programs 
2 
proposed by WE ACT AGREE are required for CLCPA 
3 
compliance.  Moreover, additional energy efficiency 
4 
incentives for induction cooking are not warranted for 
5 
three reasons. First, the Company already provides 
6 
customers in LMI-qualifying multifamily buildings 
7 
incentives to encourage replacing gas stoves with electric 
8 
induction cooking through the Affordable Multifamily 
9 
Energy Efficiency Program (“AMEEP”). Second, as proposed, 
10 
the HEMR allows LMI-qualifying buildings undertaking 
11 
heating electrification upgrades to receive incentives to 
12 
cover up to 100 percent of the cost of the behind-the-
13 
meter electrical upgrades required to connect new 
14 
induction cooking appliances, thus removing another 
15 
barrier to installing induction cooking appliances.  
16 
Third, induction cooking appliances provide limited energy 
17 
savings, which do not warrant increased EE program 
18 
incentives.  
19

Please refer to our discussion of Low- to Moderate Income 
1

customers in LMI-qualifying buildings and DACs.

B. Customer Recommendation and Analysis Tools 
4 
Q. 
Please explain the Customer Recommendation and Analysis 
5 
(“CR&A”) Tools project. 
6 
A. 
The CR&A Tools will help customers engage in the Clean 
7 
Energy transition through: (1) personalized technology and 
8 
rate option recommendations, (2) an electrification hub 
9 
that educates stakeholders on the electrification process, 
10 
(3) a contractor hub that provides contractors with 
11 
information about the Company’s clean energy programs, and 
12 
(4) tools that help employees engage with customers and 
13 
contractors throughout their clean energy journey. 
14 
Q. 
Staff (SCEPP, pp. 21-27) eliminates the CR&A Tools program 
15 
as premature because the Company has not completed the 
16 
planning phase for this project. Do you agree?  
17 
A.  
No. First, Staff’s opposition to the program based on the 
18 
incomplete status of the planning phase is unreasonable.  
19 
Projects can be, and have been, approved in rate cases 
20 


--- Page 14 ---
CASES 22-E-0064 & 22-G-0065 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
14 
 
before the planning phase was complete.  Here, the Company 
1 
has provided the Commission and parties appropriate 
2 
information to understand both the need for the program 
3 
and its costs and benefits.  The Commission does not need 
4 
all details to be complete before approving a project.  
5 
Second, Staff’s proposal would effectively delay the 
6 
project by three years even though the planning stage is 
7 
expected to be finished before the Commission acts in this 
8 
proceeding.  In discovery, the Company noted that its 
9 
Phase 0 assessment with a Benefit Cost Analysis (“BCA”) 
10 
will be completed by November 2022. Phase 0 includes a 
11 
project implementation roadmap, recommendations on 
12 
internally developed tools and third-party products, and a 
13 
cost breakdown.  A three-year delay will affect the 
14 
success of many of the Company’s Clean Energy programs, 
15 
risking CLCPA goals.  
16 
Q. 
Can you provide examples of what the CR&A Tools will do 
17 
that is not available now? 
18 
A. 
Yes.  The project is expected to provide customers with 
19 
easy-to-understand, bundled solution recommendations, such 
20 
as pairing heat pumps with building envelope insulation 
21 


--- Page 15 ---
CASES 22-E-0064 & 22-G-0065 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
15 
 
and/or air sealing, that increase energy savings, reduce 
1 
customer costs, and improve home comfort.  This particular 
2 
pairing also has the potential to reduce heat pump 
3 
capacity requirements, thus, reducing grid demand.   
4 
Currently, many LMI-qualifying building owners lack easy 
5 
access to information and tools for building 
6 
electrification, guidance on electrical upgrades, access 
7 
to contractor networks, and details on electrification or 
8 
make-ready incentives.  This tool is expected to assist 
9 
these building owners with this type of information.   
10 
Q.  
Staff questions the Company’s use of the Digital Customer 
11 
Experience (“DCX”) as a benchmark for estimating the costs 
12 
of the CR&A Tools program, and expresses concerns about 
13 
the program’s potential cost, noting that it does not yet 
14 
have a project plan.  Please explain why the CR&A Tools 
15 
should be equivalent in scale and cost to the DCX 
16 
program.   
17 
A.  
The Company’s DCX program develops and continuously 
18 
improves customer self-service capabilities, like 
19 
payments, payment assistance, and start/stop service.   
20 


--- Page 16 ---
CASES 22-E-0064 & 22-G-0065 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
16 
 
The CR&A Tools project will expand on the DCX experience 
1 
and deliver an improved and customized customer clean 
2 
energy experience.  
3 
The CR&A Tools project could eventually exceed the costs 
4 
of DCX, given the myriad of capabilities and services it 
5 
will provide to customers and contractors.  But these 
6 
benefits are crucial to obtaining CLCPA and 
7 
electrification objectives. For example, in addition to 
8 
the benefits described above, the CR&A Tools will provide 
9 
customers with insights on value of partnering 
10 
electrification with targeted rate options.  Clean energy 
11 
technologies could garner the unwarranted reputation of 
12 
increasing heating bills after switching from gas because 
13 
customers are not taking advantage of the rate option that 
14 
makes heating electrification more cost effective.   The 
15 
Company will provide education in this regard, but the 
16 
best approach is for customers to have tools to evaluate 
17 
their options.  
18

Did Staff make any other recommendations for the CR&A 
19 
Tools? 
20

A. 
Yes. Staff (SCEPP, pp. 25-26) recommends revenue

13

C. DER Make-Ready Program for Disadvantaged Communities and 
16 
Low-Income Customers 
17 
Q.  Please summarize the Company’s DER Make-Ready Program for 
18 
Disadvantaged Communities and Low-Income Customers (“DER 
19 
Make-Ready Program”).   
20 


--- Page 18 ---
CASES 22-E-0064 & 22-G-0065 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
18 
 
A.  The DER Make-Ready Program would provide capital support 
1 
toward the utility-side interconnection costs associated 
2 
with DERs that are not community distributed generation 
3 
(“CDG”). Program participation eligibility requires that 
4 
DERs be located in a DAC or in a building where at least 
5 
25 percent of the residents participate in the Company’s 
6 
Energy Affordability Program (“EAP”).   
7 
Program support is broken into three categories (small, 
8 
medium, or large) based on nameplate capacity of the DER 
9 
being developed and capped at a specified maximum for each 
10 
category.  
11 
Q. 
Did parties comment on the DER Make-Ready Program for DACs 
12 
and Low-Income Customers program?   
13 
A.  
Yes.  Three parties commented.  Staff’s SCEPP panel does 
14 
not support the Company’s proposal, whereas NRDC and WE 
15 
ACT AGREE support the proposal.   
16 
Q. 
What is Staff’s position on the DER Make-Ready Program?  
17 
A. 
Staff (SCEPP, p. 34) asserts that the program is 
18 
duplicative of ongoing statewide efforts discussed in the 
19 


--- Page 19 ---
CASES 22-E-0064 & 22-G-0065 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
19 
 
Cost Sharing 2.0 Order1 and 2022 NY-Sun Order2 to improve 
1 
market conditions for DER developers and may have 
2 
deleterious effects if combined with such programs. As 
3 
such, Staff suggests the Company explore the DER Make-
4 
Ready Program in the Cost Sharing 2.0 proceeding.   
5 
Q. 
What is the Cost Sharing 2.0 proceeding? 
6 
A. 
The Cost Sharing 2.0 proceeding addresses interconnection 
7 
cost allocation between utilities and developers.   
8 
Q. 
Does the Cost Sharing 2.0 proceeding address cost sharing 
9 
for interconnections in DACs? 
10 
A. 
No.  The Cost Sharing 2.0 Order neither includes targeted 
11 
DAC programs nor specifically mentions spurring 
12 
development projects located in these communities or in 
13 
those supporting low-income customers. 
14 
 
1 Case 20-E-0543, Petition of Interconnection Policy Working Group Seeking a 
Cost-Sharing Amendment to the New York State Standardized 
Interconnection Requirements ("Cost Sharing 2.0 Proceeding”), Order

Table 1: FTE job description 
1 
Job Title 
Description  
Engineering/Operation 
Support 
Engineering and operation support will complete design 
and execute the project. This work includes program 
management (procurement, contracting, vendor management, 
specifications setting), on-site training, maintenance 
and safety. They manage the procedural integration of 
the energy storage system into the utility network and 
operation, developing battery operation specifications, 
market operations, emergency response plans, and 
SCADA/Human Machine Interface screens. 
Substation Operator 
(shiftwork 24/7/365) 
The substation operator maintains operations of the 
system in real time grid conditions, holds electrical 
outage permits to perform work, and switches “non-
standard” electrical equipment. [Operators cannot be 
shared among projects due to union rules (Local 1&2 and 
Local 3)] 
System Operator 
The system operator remotely operates the energy storage 
system from Energy Control Center with operational 
jurisdiction over system controls, dispatches and 
charges, the asset and oversees New York Independent 
System Operator (“NYISO”) market participation.

Q.

3

5

6

7

10

11

12

13 
enrolled in pay-for-performance demand response programs. 
14

15 
25 percent during at least one of the four-hour CSRP

19

Q. 
How will Con Edison’s proposal incentivize the Company to 
1 
maximize the number of customers enrolled while also 
2

3 
A.

4 
the BCA. The 25 percent performance factor criteria will 
5 
ensure the Company is only incentivized for customers who 
6 
provide load relief. The program pays based on performance 
7 
and historically has a BCA ratio above 1. The BCA 
8 
considers the net benefits we receive from these 
9 
additional customers, which outweigh the costs. Every 
10 
incremental customer pays for itself. Because these 
11 
programs are paid for based on performance, every kW is 
12 
cost effective. Furthermore, adding additional FTEs to 
13 
support the expected growth of the DR programs increases 
14 
the absolute net benefits for customers regardless of the 
15 
presence of a DR EAM.

15

17

18

19

20 
Q. 
What is the objective of the Company’s MRP? 
21 


--- Page 158 ---
CASES 22-E-0064 & 22-G-0065 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
158 
 
A. 
The objective of Con Edison’s MRP is to reduce leak-prone 
1 
pipe (“LPP”) on the gas distribution system in a way that 
2 
maintains safety and reliability for customers. In the 
3 
current rate period (2020-22), Con Edison has a goal to 
4 
remove 270 miles of LPP using authorized budget. 
5 
Q. 
How is the proposed EAM going to incentivize outcomes that 
6 
are additional and complementary to the objective of the 
7 
MRP? 
8 
A. 
The Gas System Footprint EAM incentivizes the Company to 
9 
reduce the length of the pipe that is installed when 
10 
replacing LPP.  
11 
Q. 
Will the outcomes that the Gas System Footprint EAM is 
12 
incentivizing require a change in the Company’s approach 
13 
to the MRP? 
14 
A. 
Yes. The Company will need to adjust its approach to 
15 
targeting locations for LPP replacement to achieve both 
16 
the objectives of the MRP (remove LPP) and the objective 
17 
of the Gas System Footprint EAM (minimize pipe replacing 
18 
LPP). 
19 


--- Page 159 ---
CASES 22-E-0064 & 22-G-0065 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
159 
 
Q. 
Please explain the current approach the Company is using 
1 
to target locations with LPP for replacement in the MRP.  
2 
A. 
The Company’s current MRP approach involves a risk-based 
3 
prioritization of LPP for replacement. Once the Company 
4 
chooses certain parts of the gas system for replacement, 
5 
it generally also replaces LPP in the broader geographic 
6 
area surrounding this part of the gas system. This 
7 
approach achieves cost synergies from doing work in a 
8 
broader geographic location, as well as reduces any 
9 
impacts on customer experience for the duration of the 
10 
work (e.g., from construction work on the street). 
11 
Q. 
Please explain how the Company’s approach to target 
12 
locations with LPP for replacement will change with the 
13 
introduction of the Gas System Footprint EAM. 
14 
A. 
Not all gas pipe layouts are suitable for simplification 
15 
(i.e., simplified main design that reduces the footprint 
16 
of installed pipe). Currently, the Company is pursuing 
17 
layout simplification within the broad geographic areas 
18 
that are chosen for LPP replacement, when possible.  
19 


--- Page 160 ---
CASES 22-E-0064 & 22-G-0065 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
160 
 
With the introduction of the Gas System Footprint EAM, the 
1 
Company will need to adapt its approach to LPP targeting 
2 
in way that will consider the potential for layout 
3 
simplification. This may mean that the Company will no 
4 
longer pursue the broad geographic approach and will 
5 
instead use a more targeted approach to target LPP that 
6 
are suitable for simplification. 
7 
Q. 
Please describe the implications of the change in the 
8 
Company’s approach to target locations with LPP for 
9 
replacement with the introduction of the Gas System 
10 
Footprint EAM. 
11 
A. 
Overall, a change in the Company’s approach to maximize 
12 
simplification in LPP replacement will make MRP 
13 
achievement more challenging to meet in the short term. 
14 
Specifically, simplification projects are typically more 
15 
complex in nature, requiring additional work to complete 
16 
service transfers. A change in the Company’s approach as 
17 
described above will require additional labor from gas and 
18 
planning engineers, who will need to study pipe layouts at 
19 


--- Page 161 ---
CASES 22-E-0064 & 22-G-0065 
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
161 
 
a broader scale to identify opportunities for 
1 
simplification.  
2 
The Company will also need to adjust its approach to the 
3 
LPP replacement work so that the impact on the cost 
4 
synergies that are achieved with the current broad 
5 
geographic approach is managed.  
6 
Even though less pipe is installed in projects with 
7 
simplification, it is not always the case that such 
8 
projects come at a lower overall cost, and based on 
9 
today’s LPP targeting approach, the Company will always 
10 
look for ways to minimize the cost for LPP replacement. 
11 
With the introduction of the Gas System Footprint EAM, the 
12 
Company will need to balance cost and complexity with 
13 
meeting the goal of reducing gas system footprint. 
14 
Q.  
Why is it important for the Company to pursue a reduction 
15

A. 
New York State has set a goal for a decarbonized economy

18 
is uncertain, however the Company recognizes the 
19 
importance of efficient infrastructure investment in the 
20 


--- Page 162 ---
CASES 22-E-0064 & 22-G-0065

short term, as pathways for the future of the gas system

Question No. :495 
 
1.     For calendar years 2017 through 2021: 
a.     Provide the annual budgeted and actual number of relay upgrades with and without SCADA 
capabilities, separately; 
b.     Explain any variation between the budgeted and actual number of relay upgrades 
completed;  
c.     Provide the annual unit cost for relay upgrades with and without SCADA capabilities, 
separately; 
d.     Provide the total number of times a feeder was alive-on-backfeed (ABF). How many of 
these ABF instances would have been prevented if the NWP was installed with a modern relay 
from this program? 
2.     For calendar years 2022 through 2026, provide the forecasted annual number of relay 
upgrades and the annual unit cost. This information should be provided for relays with and 
without SCADA capabilities, separately. 
3.     On page 36, of Exhibit___(IT-5), it states that “to date, the Company has replaced 3,829 
relays with 1,336 of these equipped with SCADA capability out of approximately 27,000 NWP 
in the secondary grid.” 
a.     Explain why only 1,336 of the 3,829 relays replaced have SCADA capability. 
b.     Explain how the Company determines which NWP will receive SCADA capable relays. 
c.     Does the Company plan to replace the relays in all 27,000 NWP in the secondary grid? If 
so, in what year will the Company finish upgrading all NWP? 
d.     Do the non-SCADA capable relays have any type of communication system? If so, explain 
the communication system these relays use and how it is different from the SCADA enabled 
communications. 
4.     On page 36, of Exhibit___(IT-5), it states that “the Company plans to continue this program 
in an effort to address each location that is or becomes Distributed Generation constrained as 
well as all locations with persistent ABF issues.” Why are locations where developers have 
shown interest in installing DER through communication with the company or withdrawn 
interconnection applications not a factor? 
5.     Explain why the capital funding request specifically for modernizing protective relays in the 
rate years, 2023 through 2025, is significantly higher than the 2022 budget. 
 
 
Response

b. Most variations occurred during the first three years of the program, when actual number of relay 
upgrades completed was lower than the number of planned upgrades. This was primarily due to 
the project’s planning and initiation activities, including hardware design and testing, 
procurement lead times, personnel training, and cyber security review of software and devices.  
 
c. The table below shows the annual unit costs for relay upgrades. These program costs were not 
tracked on a per-unit basis for rate years 2017-2021, so the unit costs reflect the program costs 
for relay upgrades with and without SCADA capabilities together.

project’s costs were reduced through efficiencies. Unit cost average is also dependent on factors, 
like site complexity, that can cause variations in labor and hardware costs across multiple 
locations.  
 
d.  In the last five years, there have been an average of 727 alive on backfeed (ABF) conditions per 
year (see table below). The Company did not propose or design this program to eliminate ABF. 
While these ABF conditions would not have been prevented if the locations were SCADA-
equipped, SCADA functionality allows the Company to resolve these conditions faster and 
without dispatching crews to manually operate the equipment. This allows the Company to 
prioritize system restoration as discussed in response to question 3b.

*The actual number of NWPs at individual locations varies. The program prioritizes based on grid 
locations so actual NWP numbers are not known until projects are completed. For planning 
purposes, the Company  assumes that each location is a 3 Network Protector vault for planning

3.   a. While the SCADA program represents the hardware component to remote monitoring and control 
capabilities, SCADA functionality also requires software upgrades within the Company’s 
operations environments. These operating systems are limited to how many end points can be 
monitored and operated before costs to upgrade and manage these systems increase. For this 
reason, the Company has chosen to select high-value locations, requiring remote operations 
capabilities, for SCADA functionality that fits into the software parameters and upgrade 
schedules.  Please see the answer provided in 3b for details on prioritizing these locations.  
 
b.  As dynamics at the grid edge continue to evolve, the need for flexible, remote operation will 
increase. This program represents a core operational investment that addresses near term 
operating needs while preparing the grid for future operations inclusive of higher Distributed 
Generation penetrations withing the secondary network. Currently, the Company identifies 
locations for SCADA-enabled functionality based on near term operating need. The most 
common condition benefiting from SCADA functionality is known as Alive on Backfeed or 
“ABF.” This is a condition whereby the Network Protector does not operate to clear a potential 
Exhibit___(CES-12)
Page 5 of 55


--- Page 168 ---
 
Page 4 of 5 
fault in the distribution system. This allows a potentially faulted condition to continue until an 
operating crew can be dispatched to that location for a manual operation of the device. The 
ability to remotely operate these devices from the Control Center eliminates the need to dispatch 
emergency crews to the location and allows time to be prioritized to system restoration efforts. 
Certain areas of the grid prove to be more prone to ABF conditions and these areas are 
prioritized for the SCADA functionality.  As ABF-prone installations are addressed, the 
Company continues to need this program to prepare the grid for the future 
 
c. The Company intends to address all locations, prioritizing the most suitable locations for 
installations. The current timeframe for this brings the program’s conclusion to 2048.  
 
d. The non-SCADA relay installations operate on the same net-RMS system that the Network 
protector does. Through this system, the Company measures the following components:  
 Phase voltage for three phases 
 Flags including oil level 
 Pressure  
 Temperature 
 Load based on the current  
 NWP status open/close  
 
The SCADA system brings back the following points:  
 Analog values including: 
o Phase voltage on both the network and transformer sides 
o Phase currents 
o Total power values 
o Pressure, temperature, and oil level for newer locations 
o Load based on the current (amps) 
 Status points including: 
o NWP status - open/close  
o Relay Mode 
o Status pertaining to automatic operation of the network protector 
such as: 
 Voltage Differential too low to close 
 Phase angle wrong for closing 
 Float condition 
 Calling for a trip 
 Calling for a close 
 Fail to trip 
 Fail to close 
 
4.      The Company consider developer projects in performing these relay upgrades. The Company 
prioritizes areas for relay upgrades that have become constrained due to higher penetrations of 
Distributed Generation (“DG”) as well as locations where the Standard Interconnection 
Requirements (“SIR”) queue information indicates substantial growth in DG. To the extent that 
an individual project in the Company’s interconnection queue overlaps with these identified 
Exhibit___(CES-12)
Page 6 of 55


--- Page 169 ---
 
Page 5 of 5 
areas of benefit, the Company has taken these those projects into account and accelerated 
installation of a relay to support interconnection for these projects. In the case of SCADA being 
prioritized for ABF conditions, these benefits do not overlap with the needs of projects in the 
interconnection queue.  
 
5.     The increased funding request for rate years 2023 through 2025 is driven by two factors. First, the 
Company has moved towards submersible designs for climate proofing locations equipped with 
both SCADA and Relays. Second, supply chain and procurement issues that are a direct result of 
COVID-19 disruptions have increased costs.

Page 1 of 7

Response 
1.  
a. The Company has requested amounts that would be treated a regulatory assets, 
not O&M funding, in Exhibit __ (CES-1) for the Heating Electrification Make 
Ready program. The requested regulatory asset funding would be used to procure 
contract services to administer the program. See sheet Q1 in the attached 
workbook for a breakdown of the funding request by contract service. The 
contract services detailed in sheet Q1 would be managed by the Company’s Clean 
Heat program team.  All Clean Heat O&M labor requests are detailed in updated 
Exhibit __ (CES-6) Energy Efficiency & Targeted Demand Management O&M in 
response to DPS-13-417.    
b. The Company assumed HEMR program administration costs to be 15% of total 
program budget. To develop this assumption, the Company started with the 2021 
NENY Non-LMI EE portfolio spending on administration categories, which 
totaled to ~19% of total program spending. See sheet Q1 of the attached 
workbook for table summarizing portfolio spending by category. Next, the 
Company removed categories which are not applicable to the HEMR (e.g., energy 
savings measurement and evaluation). Finally, the Company estimates marketing 
Exhibit___(CES-12)
Page 18 of 55


--- Page 181 ---
 
Page 5 of 8 
synergies with the Clean Heat program and as a result will require a smaller share 
of marketing as a fraction of total budget (assuming 1% as a share of total budget 
for HEMR compared to 3% as a share of total budget for the 2021 NENY Non-
LMI EE portfolio). 
2.  
a. The $240,000 for each of 2023, 2024, and 2025 is solely for labor costs.  
Currently, the Company envisions using two resources --one internal and one 
external, contracted resource.  This may be revised during program development. 
The internal resource is expected to cost between $90,000 and $130,000, with the 
remainder for external support.  
b. The $240,000 per year funding request is solely for labor costs. 
c.  
Yes, the Company is requesting resource dollars to fund one additional full-time 
employee to serve as the Program Manager.   
 
This employee would be hired in the Specialist job family (Specialist, Senior 
Specialist or Project Specialist) at a salary range of $90,000 - $130,000 with an 
expected hire date of 1/1/2023. This new position will work proactively with 
external stakeholders including NYSERDA, the City of New York, the New York 
Solar Energy Industries Association (NYSEIA), local developers and community 
advocates to assist in program implementation in a business development role.  
The individual will identify areas of interconnection opportunity for customers in 
disadvantaged communities and/or low-income customers.  The position will also 
coordinate with NYSERDA in their pre-development efforts with developers in 
these communities as identified in the 10GW Solar Roadmap White Paper.1 
 
The FTE resources in the existing workforce are dedicated to supporting the 
interconnection process and maintaining compliance with state requirements. 
Repurposing these resources would negatively impact SIR performance as the 
effort associated with the DER MR Program is different from the traditional 
interconnection process and warrants a separation of duties. 
 
d. The funding assumption utilized is one FTE in the specialist family and associated 
salary and one contractor position for program support. See response to parts a 
and b.   
 
e. If approved, the program is planned to commence no later than March 31, 2023. 
3.  
a. See sheet Q3 of the attached workbook.   
b. See sheet Q3 of the attached workbook. 
c. See sheet Q3 of the attached workbook.  
                                                 
1 Case 21-E-0629, In the Matter of the Advancement of Distributed Solar, New York’s 10 GW 
Distributed Solar Roadmap: Policy Options for Continued Growth in Distributed Solar (filed 
December 17, 2021).

--- Page 182 ---
 
Page 6 of 8 
d. No incremental employees are requested at this time in association with the IPP 
funding request. 
e. In order to forecast the funding requirements for fiscal years 2023-2025, the 
Company calculated the total O&M costs for years 2019 through 2021, plus the 
projected costs for 2022. The total O&M costs for this time period is $14.2 
million, or an average of $3.55 million per year. 
Assuming that O&M activities remain are the same between 2023 and 2025, a 
$3.55 million annual cost would equate to a funding requirement of $10.65 
million for the rate years 2023-2025. However, the Company anticipates enrolling 
25% more customers in Wave 4 than were enrolled in Waves 1-3. This is 
projected to add an additional $2.66 million to O&M costs, which would bring the 
new total to $13.31 million.  
Through subsequent discussions with the vendors contracted to support the IPP,  
the Company was able to identify $660,000 in O&M reductions. These were 
primarily achieved through decisions to:  
 
• 
Use Con Edison’s call center during the Wave 4 recruitment period,  
• 
Modify the welcome packages sent to customers upon enrollment, and  
• 
Eliminate reports produced by vendors that were found to be unnecessary. 
 
The Company also factored in an additional $2.65 million needed to prepare for a 
potential future expansion of demand-based rates to a larger population of 
customers (if such expansion is not pursued, the Company will return the funding 
requested for this purpose to customers).  This, and the O&M reductions 
described above, results in the three-year funding request of $15.3 million. 
 
4. The difference in values between Exhibit___(CES-5) and the workpaper “AP-EG3 
Company Labor Program Changes is due to escalation.  Exhibit___(CES-5) is produced 
using Con Edison’s ePBCS budgeting system which takes the number of FTE by Title 
and Section and applies salary rates, escalation, and, as appropriate, other factors to arrive 
at the projected annual labor cost in each year.  The calculations are programmed into the 
system and uniformly applied.  By contrast, the Exhibit AP-EG3 worksheet uses 
unadjusted salary data based on the 2021 Salary Rate. 
5.  
a. The O&M estimates for the future sites were calculated using a combined top-
down and bottom-up approach. The battery maintenance contracts for the 
proposed sites were based on the existing battery maintenance contracts for 98th 
Street and Fox Hills, accounting for the fact that it may not be the same battery 
vendor serving all sites. The cost of the secure communication line is known from 
other existing Company installations, and was applied directly to each of the 
proposed sites. There are also non-battery related service contracts associated 
with this site. However, as all proposed locations are within substations, the 
Company will leverage existing service contracts for fire alarm systems, cleaning, 
vegetation control, and snow removal, avoiding any additional cost. The training 
Exhibit___(CES-12)

Page 7 of 8 
cost is based on a combination of our past experience with 98th Street and on 
battery vendor quotes for Fox Hills. The budget for FTE is explained in more 
detail in 5d. 
The Fox Hills site, while still being constructed has incurred the following O&M 
expenses: 
 Geotechnical survey and remediation expense for 2021 and 2022: 
$102,830 
 Fox Hill secure communication landline quote: $24,000 (annually) 
 Battery maintenance agreement starting at $104,637 (annually) 
The breakdown of O&M expenses for our future sites derived from the experience 
from 98th Street and Fox Hills is provided on sheet Q5 of the attached workbook.  
b.  See sheet Q5 for the breakdown of funding by the categories requested. 
c.  See sheet Q5 for the breakdown of funding by the categories requested. 
d.  See the Company’s response to DPS-21-618 for a detailed explanation of the 
incremental employees requested including titles, salary, and duties. Currently, 
the Company does not have dedicated resources to operate energy storage systems 
on its substations. Starting with Fox Hills, the day-to-day operation will be 
managed by the Company’s Senior System Operator at the Energy Control Center 
(ECC), and since this is outside the normal operation for the ECC, it will require 
additional staff. Not all Company substations are manned with operators. Existing 
resources have no additional time to take on this added work.  As such, additional 
substation operators are required to perform battery systems oversight. See sheet 
Q5 of the attached workbook for a summary of the future staffing needs. 
e. See 5a of this response.

Response 
In Exhibit___(CES-5), page 2, the Company states, “currently the Company only has seven 
people dedicated to energy storage in the bulk storage and utility sited groups.” 
 
1. For each of the employees currently dedicated to energy storage projects, provide:  
a. Job title; See Table 1 below.  
b. Salary; See Table 1 below. Total compensation for the seven existing employees 
is $1,121,240. The average compensation per employee is $160,177. 
c. A detailed list of the employee’s job duties; See Table 1 below. 
d. Whether the employee is assigned to Company-owned or external energy storage 
projects. 
i. For employees assigned to internal energy storage projects, explain 
whether the employee is assigned to manage the development of a 
particular energy storage project, or assigned to operate and coordinate 
maintenance of operational projects, as described on pages 82, line 18 
through page 83, line 2, of the CES Panel’s direct testimony; The four 
people assigned to internal energy storage projects, are working on the 
operation and maintenance of the 98th Street / Ozone Park site, 
engineering, procurement, construction and commissioning of the Fox 
Hills and Nevins Street projects, and the ideation, planning, and execution 
of future internal energy storage projects.

2. Provide an organizational chart of the Energy Storage group, including the sixteen FTEs 
requested in this proceeding.  
 
 
Figure 1: Energy Storage Organization org chart with existing and new employees.  The new 
storage organization would combine the two existing storage groups, with the new hires, into a 
single dedicated storage group, consistent with the Roland Berger recommendation.  The 
reporting hierarchy will be determined later in 2022.

In Exhibit___(CES-5), pages 1-6, the Company requests ten FTEs: three for project Ideation, 
four for Planning and Execution, and three for Operations (Market Development and 
Implementation FTEs). 
 
1. For each Market Development and Implementation FTE requested, provide:   
a. Job title; See Table 1. 
b. Salary; See Table 1. 
c. A detailed list of employee job duties; See Table 1. 
d. Whether the employee is assigned to Ideation, Planning and Execution, or 
Operations; Please see employee breakdown per the referenced section in the 
testimony and table 1 above.  
e. Anticipated hiring date. See Table 1. 
2. Explain how the Company developed its proposed headcount and schedule as shown on 
page 4 of Exhibit___(CES-5).  
 
The Company intends to develop a dedicated storage organization to share knowledge, prioritize 
how to proactively deploy resources to maximize the benefits from and installations of energy 
storage, and be able to execute on all business models and projects we deploy, including 
NYSERDA’s anticipated roadmap.  The staffing and ramp up is based on the Company’s 
estimate of the resources needed to have the ideation, planning, execution, and operations in 
order to reach our share of the energy storage goals over the next decade.  Given the nature of 
New York City as a load pocket that historically has required the operation of local fossil fueled 
peaking units to maintain reliability, significant amounts of energy storage will be needed to both 
balance intermittent renewables and satisfy contingencies and respond to outages.  As indicated 
in Exhibit_(CES-5), three FTEs will develop and launch new storage programs to create new 
delivery channels, four FTEs will facilitate overall storage adoption by identifying potential 
transmission and distribution interconnections and siting locations, and the remaining three will 
focus on optimizing the operations of the batteries to maximize wholesale and distribution 
benefits that will help lower customer costs. 
The Company’s requesting staffing is in line with a recent report performed by Roland Berger, 
based on benchmarking with other organizations and the Company’s internal goal of attracting 
750 MW of energy storage by 2025, that between 22 and 25 FTEs will be needed.  The executive 
summary of that report is attached. 
On pages 82, line 18 through page 83, line 2, of the CES Panel’s direct testimony, the Company 
states “four FTEs will manage the proposed utility-owned storage projects… the remaining two 
FTEs will operate and coordinate maintenance of these new projects as they come online.” 
Further, Table 12 on page 83 of the CES Panel’s direct testimony provides a requested headcount 
of 4 FTEs in 2023, 5 in 2024, and 6 in 2025.  Exhibit___(CES-5), pages 7-16 (Energy Storage 
Installation and Operations project), mentions requiring FTEs but does not include specific 
numbers. 
Exhibit___(CES-12)
Page 36 of 55


--- Page 199 ---
 
Page 7 of 7 
3. Confirm that the costs of the six FTEs described on pages 82-83 of the CES Panel’s 
testimony are included in the Capital and O&M requests shown on pages 15-16 of 
Exhibit___(CES-5). Yes, there are included.  
4. For each of the six internal energy storage project FTEs requested, provide: 
a. Job title; See Table 1. 
b. Salary; See Table 1. 
c. A detailed list of employee job duties; See Table 1. 
d. Which of the four proposed Company-owned energy storage projects the 
employee will be assigned to, or whether the employee will be assigned to operate 
and coordinate maintenance of these new projects as they come online;  
Of the six FTEs, four will be assigned to one of the four new projects, so there 
will be one employee per site. The remaining two FTEs will support the 
commissioning of all projects in the proposed portfolio. After which they will 
transition into the dispatch, operation, and maintenance of the portfolio. Because 
of the multi-discipline nature of these projects, the existing employee will be 
supporting the new FTEs in the project management and execution. Based on 
current experience, each project required a minimum of two FTEs to successfully 
execute and integrate these systems into the utility architecture.  
e. Whether the employee’s costs are included in the Capital Elements of Expense 
table on page 15 of Exhibit___(CES-5) or the Ongoing Maintenance Expense 
table on page 16 of Exhibit___(CES-5); and Employee cost are accounted for in 
the Ongoing Maintenance Expense table on page 16 of Exhibit___(CES-5) 
f. Anticipated hiring date. See Table 1. 
5. For each of the four FTEs assigned to development of a utility-owned energy storage 
project, explain what role the employee will provide once such project is operational.  
All FTE’s will assist in making newly commissioned assets ready for dispatch throughout 
the year. Employees will coordinate maintenance work with both Company forces and 
vendors. At the same time, FTE’s will develop and monitor performance metrics 
associated with these projects.  This includes closing out all punchlist items post 
commissioning, state of health monitoring and dispatch for pre and post summer 
operations, managing vendor maintenance agreement and warranty claims, provide 
subject matter expert support for operating groups as they get acclimated with the new 
asset and serve at the interface between the Company, NYISO, warranty vendors and 
operators.

2
New York regulators have set a goal of deploying 3,000 MW of 
energy storage by 2030
New York's Energy Storage Roadmap
Source: Desk research, Roland Berger
> Quantify and attribute locational and time-of-day values to energy storage 
resources
> Improve financeablity of storage projects leveraging utility balance sheets where 
possible
> Authorize $350 million in bridge incentives to accelerate the energy storage market
> Require that New York’s investor-owned utilities hold competitive procurements 
for a minimum of 350 MW of bulk-sited energy storage
> Reflect the environmental benefits and resiliency energy storage brings to the 
grid in benefit-cost analyses
> Enable distributed energy resources to meet distribution system and wholesale 
system needs more cost effectively for ratepayers
> Work with local AHJs to streamline permitting and siting challenges and reduce 
the non-hardware costs of energy storage
Key recommendations
Timeline
ConEd is expected to take a leading role ensuring that the state meets the 
mandates of the Energy Storage Roadmap
June 2018: NYSERDA 
releases NY Energy Storage 
Roadmap
2030: Deadline to hit goal of 
3,000 MW of storage deployed
Dec. 2018: NY PSC order 
codifies Energy Storage 
Roadmap recommendations
2025: Interim goal of 1,500 MW 
deployed
2019: NYSERDA files 
implementation plan with the 
NY PSC

8
Our recommended storage organization aligns with four key 
principles based on internal, external interviews and RB expertise
Key principles
Adequate resources for current and future state should be designed into the 
organization from the outset – Certain capabilities and skillsets are design agnostic
Roles and responsibilities across the project lifecycle are well defined –
repeatable processes (e.g., contract management) can be standardized
Senior executive buy in and care for the growth of energy storage is critically 
importantly in motivating and aligning shared resources toward a common goal
A clear strategic vision for energy storage – across use cases – should be 
established and articulated early
Guiding principles for organizational design

Comments

– The leads and project managers support in the 
formulation, execution and ongoing operation of 
energy storage projects
> The organizational structure provides CECONY
flexibility and optionality as the:
– Size and number of BESS projects grows
– Ownership model for BESS changes
– Regulatory landscape shifts

HR, financial planning, etc. - are considered separate 
and not shown in this proposed organizational 
structure
> This structure is based on ConEd's current sales

requirements will increase beyond those depicted
> ConEd may seek to address limited spans of control in

Company Name: Con Edison 
Case Description:  2022 Con Ed Electric & Gas Rate Cases 
Cases: 22-E-0064 & 22-G-0065 
 
 
Response to DPS Interrogatories – Set DPS- 22 
Date of Response: April 05, 2022 
Responding Witness: Customer Energy Solutions Panel 
 
 
Question No. :653 
 
Subject:  Expected Useful Life of EE and Heat Pump Equipment 
  
Refer to pages 116-117, of the direct testimony of the Customer Energy Services Panel (CES 
Panel). In the CES Panel’s direct testimony, the Company proposes to continue to use a 10-year 
amortization period for Electric and Gas EE programs and to change the amortization period for 
the Clean Heat program to 15 years. The Company states that these amortization periods are 
consistent with the average lifetimes of the equipment, supported by the programs. 
1.     How does the Company expect the average Expected Useful Life of its Electric and Gas EE 
and Clean Heat programs to change over time, as the emphasis of the Company’s portfolios 
shifts from traditional EE investments to electrification and deeper weatherization measures? 
Provide expected average EULs across the Company’s Electric and Gas EE and Clean Heat 
portfolios from 2022 through 2026. 
 
 
Response 
 
The tables below give expected useful life (EUL) forecasts for the electric EE and gas EE 
portfolios through 2025, and for the Clean Heat portfolio through 2022.  
 
Portfolio Expected 
Useful Life (EUL) 
2022 
2023 
2024 
2025 
2026 
Electric EE 
8.99 
8.86 
9.44 
10.12 
-- 
Gas EE 
12.27 
12.32 
12.36 
12.13 
-- 
Clean Heat 
15.24 
-- 
-- 
-- 
-- 
 
The electric and gas EE portfolios include savings associated with the Company’s low- and 
moderate-income (LMI) electric and gas EE portfolio, respectively.  
 
The Company forecasted EULs for years in which it has authorized funding. As result, the 
Company did not forecast EULs in 2026 as the New Efficiency New York (NENY) Order 
authorizes funding through 2025. 
 
The Clean Heat EUL forecast ends after 2022 because the Company forecasts to exhaust 
authorized funding in 2022 for this portion of its portfolio. The Company filed a Clean Heat 
Petition in the NENY proceeding on February 24, 2022 and awaits Commission action on that 
Exhibit___(CES-12)
Page 54 of 55


--- Page 217 ---
 
Page 2 of 2 
petition which will affect targets and budgets for its Clean Heat portfolio.  Additionally, the 
Company expects its total NENY portfolio EUL to increase towards the roughly 15-year EUL of 
the Clean Heat portfolio if the Commission authorizes additional funding through 2025 for the 
Company’s Clean Heat portfolio in the NENY Interim Review process.  
 
In addition, the Company’s electric and gas EE portfolio EULs will also increase if the 
Commission approves additional funding sufficient to support additional building envelope 
upgrades in the Interim review process. These measures can often have EULs up to 30 years for 
measures like windows.
